READ  IT  THE  WAY  TOPPERS  DO
How to use this book
One day a sitting. Read the day, then close the book and say the Five to remember out loud before you
look. That single habit is the difference between reading current affairs and scoring from them.
1
Read the day
Every item, source and
table. Underline the bold
facts: they are the exam.
2
Say the five
Close the book. Recite
the day’s Five to
remember. Only then
check.
3
Take the quiz
Each day ends with its
ten-question quiz. Note
every wrong answer.
4
Come back
Tick the tracker. Recall
the day again on day 3
and day 7, then use the
revision sheet.
Your revision tracker
Tick as you go. Day 3 and day 7 recalls are what move a fact from short-term to exam-day memory.

NABARD  CURRENT  AFFAIRS
Agriculture
1. Hanging Glaciers
Source: Indian Express
Context A study identified 219 hanging glaciers in Uttarakhand's Alaknanda basin, warning that
warming and permafrost thaw are increasing break-off risks — potentially triggering ice-rock
avalanches and outburst floods.
What a Hanging Glacier Is
A glacier that originates high on a mountain wall or valley flank
And terminates abruptly at the edge of a steep precipice or cliff face
Rather than flowing down into a main valley floor
Its snout ends high above the valley trough
Routinely shedding ice through calving, icefalls and avalanches
Where They Are Found
Globally: high-relief alpine environments — the European Alps, the Andes, the North American Rockies,
and the Himalaya-Karakoram range
In India: heavily concentrated along the steep, glaciated slopes of:
Garhwal and Kumaon Himalayas (Uttarakhand)
Himachal Pradesh
Ladakh
Sikkim
How They Form — Three Processes
High-altitude accumulation: Heavy snowfall collects in steep niches, ledges and cirques → compacts
into firn → glacial ice.
Glacial retreat: Warming causes trunk/valley glaciers to thin and retreat, disconnecting smaller
tributary glaciers → leaving them perched high on mountain walls.
Thermal destabilisation: Rising temperatures thaw permafrost and basal ice, producing meltwater
that lubricates the glacier–bedrock interface → making hanging glaciers unstable and prone to
collapse.
2. NCDEX Launches RAINCHNNAI Weather Derivative
NCDEX launched RAINCHNNAI, India’s second rainfall-based weather derivatives contract.

3. ICAR-IIOR National Consultation on Oilseed Technology
ICAR–Indian Institute of Oilseeds Research (ICAR-IIOR), Hyderabad will organise a national
consultation on 10 September 2026.
Focus is on commercialisation of oilseed technologies.
Important for oilseed productivity, technology transfer and agricultural R&D.
Schemes
1. Fisheries Infrastructure Projects under PMMSY
Fisheries infrastructure projects worth ₹36.49 crore will be launched in Puducherry.
Projects are under Pradhan Mantri Matsya Sampada Yojana (PMMSY).
Focus on sustainable livelihoods, fisheries infrastructure and value-chain efficiency.
2. Cooperative-led Seaweed Farming
A Multi-Dimensional Capacity Building Conclave will promote cooperative-led seaweed farming.
It is linked with the Blue Economy.
Focus: sustainable coastal livelihoods and strengthening the seaweed value chain.
Reports & Articles
1. Ease of Doing R&D in India — NITI Aayog
Nearly 80% of ANRF funding is concentrated in IITs.
India lacks a standardised system to track research funding, recipients and duplicate projects.
Proposed UPMS requires robust Persistent Digital Identifiers (PIDs) and metadata infrastructure.
2. 4th Indo-German Environment Forum
The 4th Indo-German Environment Forum is being held in New Delhi on 1 September 2026.
Theme: “Together For More Resilience.”
Focus: bilateral cooperation on environment and climate change.

Banking and Finance
1. India’s Forex Reserves Hit Record High
India’s foreign exchange reserves reached an all-time high of $729.3 billion.
About $47 billion was added after RBI’s swap scheme announced on 5 June.
Reserves provide approximately 11 months of import cover.

RBI  AND  NABARD  CURRENT  AFFAIRS
Economy
1. India's GDP Grows 7.8% in Q1 FY27
Source: TOI
Context India's GDP growth stood at 7.8 per cent in the April–June 2026 quarter (Q1 FY27) — quicker
than the 6.9 per cent in Q1 of last year, but slower than the 8.6 per cent of the January–March 2026
quarter.
The Figures
Indicator Q1 FY27
Real GDP growth 7.8%
Nominal GDP growth 10.3%
Real GVA growth 8.2%
Comparison: 6.9% in Q1 FY26; 8.6% in Q4 FY26
Note that GVA growth (8.2%) exceeds GDP growth (7.8%). GDP = GVA + taxes on products − subsidies.
When GDP grows slower than GVA, it usually means subsidies rose faster than product taxes —
consistent with the fertiliser subsidy overshoot.
GDP — Gross Domestic Product
GDP is the total monetary value of all final goods and services produced within a country during a
specific period, usually one year or one quarter.
Simple example
Suppose in one year India produces:
Wheat and other agricultural products → ₹10 lakh crore
Cars, steel, textiles etc. → ₹20 lakh crore
Banking, IT, transport, hotels etc. → ₹70 lakh crore
Then the country's GDP would broadly represent the ₹100 lakh crore of final economic production, after
accounting for the relevant taxes and subsidies.
GDP Formula
GDP = GVA + Taxes on products − Subsidies on products
Where:
GVA (Gross Value Added) = Output − Intermediate Consumption
So, GDP measures the value created by the economy after avoiding double counting of intermediate
goods.
Three major sectors
Agriculture, forestry & fishing

September 2026 full compilation  ·  Page 10 of 191
“Rest is part of the plan, not a break from it.”
WhatsApp channel: whatsapp.com/channel/0029Vao03aUADTO9sOoigE2F  ·  +91 87086 52887  ·  c4scourses.in  ·  free, share it on
Industry — mining, manufacturing, electricity, construction etc.
Services — banking, IT, trade, transport, real estate, public administration etc.
In India, services account for the largest share of GVA, followed by industry and agriculture.
Nominal GDP vs Real GDP
Nominal GDP
Measured at current prices
Affected by both production and price changes/inflation
Real GDP
Measured at constant prices
Removes the effect of price changes
Therefore, it is more useful for measuring actual economic growth
Example:
If GDP rises 10%, but prices have increased 6%, the economy's real output has not necessarily grown by
10%.
GDP vs GVA
GVA → measures value added by producers/sectors.
GDP → GVA plus net product taxes.
Therefore: GDP = Σ GVA + taxes on products − subsidies on products.
GDP vs NDP
NDP = GDP − depreciation (Consumption of Fixed Capital).
2. Fiscal Deficit at 26.8% of FY27 Target
India’s fiscal deficit stood at 26.8% of the full-year Budget Estimate by July-end.
Absolute fiscal deficit was ₹4.55 lakh crore.
Net tax revenue was ₹8.45 lakh crore, while expenditure stood at ₹17.62 lakh crore.
Social Issue
1. National Small Industry Day
30 August is observed as National Small Industry Day in India.
It recognises the contribution of small industries and MSMEs to employment and economic
development.
The observance is linked to the 2000 policy package for Small Scale Industries.
2. Enforced Disappearances Day
30 August is observed as the International Day of the Victims of Enforced Disappearances.
The day promotes justice, truth, accountability and victims’ rights.
Based on UNGA Resolution A/RES/65/209
3. Sone River Water-Sharing Pact
Bihar and Jharkhand signed an MoU on sharing Sone River water.
The agreement was signed in Delhi in the presence of Union Home Minister Amit Shah and the two

4. OIL Green Energy Waste-to-Clean Energy Projects
OIL Green Energy signed MoUs with Gurugram, Faridabad, Hisar and Ambala municipalities.
Projects will process 5,000 tonnes/day of municipal solid waste.
Expected output: 70–75 TPD CBG and around 50 MW green power
Misc GA
1. Kyrgyzstan is hosting the 26th Shanghai Cooperation Organisation (SCO)
Context Kyrgyzstan's capital Bishkek is hosting the 26th Shanghai Cooperation Organisation (SCO)
Summit, as the Eurasian bloc marks 25 years since its inception.
Kyrgyzstan
Officially the Kyrgyz Republic
A mountainous, landlocked sovereign republic in Central Asia
Formerly a union republic of the Soviet Union, known as Kirgiziya
Declared independence on 31 August 1991
Location: the heart of Central Asia, dominated by the Tien Shan mountain system
Capital: Bishkek, in the fertile Chu River valley in the north
Borders: Kazakhstan, China, Tajikistan and Uzbekistan
About the SCO
Founded in 2001 in Shanghai, from the earlier "Shanghai Five"
Founding members: China, Russia, Kazakhstan, Kyrgyzstan, Tajikistan, Uzbekistan
India and Pakistan joined in 2017; Iran in 2023; Belarus in 2024
Secretariat: Beijing
RATS — Regional Anti-Terrorist Structure — headquartered at Tashkent
India hosted the 2023 summit (virtual) and the SCO Heads of Government meeting
2. National Test House — 115th Foundation Day
National Test House (NTH) celebrated its 115th Foundation Day in Bengaluru.
New facilities include coal, solar water-heater and utensils testing facilities.
The NTH Suvidha mobile application was also launched.
3. Legal Metrology (Indian Standard Time) Rules, 2026
Department of Consumer Affairs notified rules making Indian Standard Time (IST) the single time
reference for official purposes.
Rules aim to improve accurate time-stamping, cybersecurity and synchronisation.
They will come into force 180 days after Gazette publication.

1. India–USA Javelin Missile Deal
Indian Army signed a $93 million agreement for nearly 100 FGM-148 Javelin ATGMs.
Javelin is a fire-and-forget, infrared-guided anti-tank missile.
The deal may support future co-production in India.
2. TASL–Javelin Co-Production MoU
Tata Advanced Systems Limited (TASL) signed an MoU with the Javelin Joint Venture.
The agreement explores final assembly and component production in India.
Javelin can engage tanks and armoured targets at ranges of up to 5 km.
3. India Gets Dialogue Partner Status in GCAP
India secured Dialogue Partner status in the Global Combat Air Programme (GCAP).
GCAP is a 6th-generation fighter programme of the UK, Italy and Japan.
India is not yet a builder or co-developer under this status.
4. CISF–DFI Counter-Drone MoU
CISF and Drone Federation of India signed an MoU to strengthen counter-drone capabilities.
Behror, Rajasthan, will be developed as a Centre of Excellence.
Focus areas include drone detection, SOPs and drone forensics.
5. Indian Navy Commissions DSV Nipun
Indian Navy commissioned its second indigenous Diving Support Vessel, Nipun.
Built by Hindustan Shipyard Limited, it has around 75% indigenous content.
Supports deep-sea diving up to 300 m and submarine rescue operations.
6. T-72/T-90 Tank Overhaul Facility
A ₹472-crore tank overhaul facility is being established at Vehicle Factory Jabalpur.
It will support MRO of India’s T-72 and T-90 tanks.
Planned capacity: 80 tanks annually.
7. Lionel Messi Retires from International Football
Lionel Messi announced his retirement from international football for Argentina on 31 August 2026,
ending a 21-year international career.
He finished as Argentina’s most-capped player and all-time leading scorer, with 207 appearances
and 125 goals.
Major achievements include the 2022 FIFA World Cup and Copa América titles in 2021 and 2024; his
final international match was the 2026 World Cup final.
He is an eight-time Ballon d’Or winner and one of the most decorated players in international
football.
Science & Technology
1. Nancy Grace Roman Space Telescope
NASA launched the Nancy Grace Roman Space Telescope aboard a SpaceX Falcon Heavy.

2. DoT–C-DOT Optical Network Testbed
DoT and C-DOT signed a 5-year agreement to establish an end-to-end optical network testbed.
It will test optical core, optical access and quantum-security technologies.
Aim: bridge the gap between indigenous R&D and commercial deployment.
3. YJ-18A Stealth-Coated Missile
China reportedly tested a new YJ-18A anti-ship cruise missile variant.
It uses a radar-absorbing coating designed to reduce radar reflections.
The missile combines subsonic cruising with a supersonic terminal attack.
4. Gangaichthys indonepalicus
Scientists discovered a new subterranean fish species in West Champaran, Bihar, near Nepal.
It is the first recorded subterranean fish from the Indo-Gangetic Basin.
Features include lack of pigmentation, reduced eyes and an elongated body.
International Affairs
1. China–Bangladesh 2+2 Dialogue
China and Bangladesh are preparing for their first 2+2 diplomatic and defence dialogue.
It will combine foreign affairs and defence-level discussions.
Important for understanding China’s growing engagement in South Asia.
2. Sone River Pact
Bihar and Jharkhand signed an agreement on Sone River water sharing.
It addresses a long-standing inter-state water-sharing issue.
Important for federalism and river-water governance.
Appointments & Resignations
1. John Ternus Becomes Apple CEO
Tim Cook stepped down as Apple CEO on 31 August 2026.
John Ternus succeeded him as CEO.
Important corporate leadership change.
2. Peyman Kargar — TVS Motor
Peyman Kargar will become TVS Motor’s CEO and Director from 27 January 2027.
He will succeed K.N. Radhakrishnan.
Kargar has over 30 years of global automotive experience.

The federation was established in 2024.

3. National Sports Day
29 August is observed as National Sports Day in memory of Major Dhyan Chand.
2026 marked his 121st birth anniversary.
Campaign: “Ek Ghanta Khel Ke Maidan Mein.”
Important Days
1. International Day Against Nuclear Tests
Observed on 29 August every year.
Declared by UNGA Resolution A/RES/64/35 in 2009.
The date marks the closure of Kazakhstan’s Semipalatinsk Nuclear Test Site in 1991.

Agriculture
1. Global Coral Reefs Under Stress: GCRMN Report
Source: Down To Earth
Context The Global Coral Reef Monitoring Network (GCRMN) released the 7th Status of Coral Reefs of
the World report — finding near-annual bleaching stress, a 9.5 per cent decline in global hard coral
cover, and recovery windows shrinking to just 4–6 years.
Key Findings
Global hard coral cover has declined by about 9.5% compared with the 1980–2009 reference period.
There has been a pronounced decline since 2010.
2023–24 witnessed the 4th Global Mass Coral Bleaching Event.
In 2024, around 87% of coral reefs experienced unprecedented heat stress.
42.6% of global reefs experienced heat stress of ≥8°C-weeks, a level associated with widespread
bleaching and mortality.
In 2025, this figure was 27.4%.
Global hard coral cover declined by 8.9% between 2023 and 2024, the largest single-year relative
decline recorded.
Coral Reefs
Coral reefs are underwater ecosystems built mainly by tiny marine animals called coral polyps.
They are found mostly in warm, shallow, clear and nutrient-poor tropical seas.
Coral polyps secrete calcium carbonate (CaCO₃), which forms the hard reef structure.
How coral reefs form
Coral polyps live together in colonies.
They build calcium-carbonate skeletons.
When corals die, their skeletons remain.
New corals grow over these structures.
Over thousands of years, this creates large coral reefs.
Why coral reefs are important
Biodiversity: Support around 25% of marine species despite covering less than 1% of the ocean floor.
Coastal protection: Reduce the impact of waves, storms and erosion.
Livelihoods: Support fisheries, tourism and coastal communities.
Carbon and nutrient cycling: Contribute to important marine ecosystem processes.
Food security: Provide fishing grounds for millions of people.

Major threats
Ocean warming → coral bleaching
Ocean acidification → makes calcium-carbonate formation difficult
Marine pollution
Overfishing
Sedimentation
Coastal development
Destructive fishing practices
What Coral Bleaching Is
When water becomes unusually warm, corals become stressed.
They expel their zooxanthellae.
The coral loses its pigments and primary nutrient supply
Turning bone-white, leaving the calcified skeleton vulnerable to disease and starvation
If stressful conditions continue for a long time, the coral may die.
2. Pichavaram Mangroves: ₹2,485 Crore Economic Value
Study estimated the Total Economic Value (TEV) of Pichavaram mangroves at ₹2,485.38 crore.
Per-hectare value: ₹1.83 crore; soil organic carbon stock is 251.14 tonnes/ha.
Total blue carbon asset value: ₹1,612.40 crore, around 65% of total economic value.
Highlights the economic importance of mangrove conservation and carbon storage.

1. Mission Rangeen Machhli 2031
Source: PIB
Context The Vice-President of India released the Mission Rangeen Machhli 2031 Strategic Action Plan
for Ornamental Fisheries Development, during a fisheries outreach programme at Agatti,
Lakshadweep.
What It Is
An initiative to develop, regulate and scale the ornamental fisheries sector
Covering the breeding, rearing and trading of decorative fish for home aquariums and commercial
displays
Into an organised, globally competitive industry
About
Implementing body: Department of Fisheries, Ministry of Fisheries, Animal Husbandry & Dairying
Location focus: Lakshadweep Islands — launched from Agatti Island
Key Features
Standardised Value Chain SOPs
Common rules for breeding, hatcheries, disease control, packaging and transport.
Ensures live fish survive transport safely.
Biosecurity & Quality
Quarantine + health checks to prevent diseases and invasive pathogens.

RBI  CURRENT  AFFAIRS
Banking and Finance
1. The Unified Payments Interface (UPI)
Source: The Hindu
Context UPI cemented its role as the backbone of India's digital economy, processing 2,366 crore
transactions worth nearly ₹30 lakh crore in July 2026 alone.
What UPI Is
A real-time interoperable payment system enabling instantaneous inter-bank fund transfers
through mobile applications
Built as open-architecture digital public infrastructure
Converts a user's bank account details into a simple Virtual Payment Address (VPA) or UPI ID
About
Developed by: the National Payments Corporation of India (NPCI)
Launched: piloted in April 2016 with 21 banks, under then-RBI Governor Raghuram Rajan — now
expanding to over 730 participating banks
Aim: build a seamless, interoperable, cashless ecosystem; bring unbanked and underbanked
populations into formal digital finance; and enable friction-free person-to-person (P2P) and person-
to-merchant (P2M) settlement.
The Three-Layer Structure
Worth fixing clearly for exams:
TPAP — Third-Party Application Provider: the app the user sees (Google Pay, PhonePe, Paytm)
PSP Bank — the bank that sponsors the TPAP and connects it to UPI
NPCI — operates the central switch, routing instructions between banks

3. PhonePe Launches UPI 123Pay for Feature Phones
PhonePe launched UPI 123Pay to enable UPI payments on feature phones without internet access.
It uses an SMS-based architecture suitable for low/2G connectivity.
Supports P2P and P2M payments, balance checks and transaction history.
Includes an AI-powered voice helpline in English and 12 Indian languages.

Social Issue
1. V-Dem Democracy Index
Source: The Hindu
Context The latest V-Dem Democracy Report placed India's Electoral Democracy Index score at its
lowest level since 1975, continuing a decline observed since 2009.
What the Index Is
A global framework measuring democracy using a multidimensional approach
Rather than treating democracy merely as the conduct of elections
Dimensions evaluated:
Electoral integrity
Civil liberties
Institutional autonomy
Judicial accountability
Freedom of expression
Public administration
About
Published by: the Varieties of Democracy (V-Dem) Institute, at the University of Gothenburg,
Sweden
Aim: measure and compare different dimensions of democracy, track progress or decline over time,
and identify trends such as democratisation and autocratisation.
Key Findings on India
Lowest since 1975: Electoral Democracy Index score at its lowest level since 1975, according to V-Dem.
Autocratisation: India is identified as undergoing slow autocratisation, marked by weakening
democratic institutions.
Institutional decline: Judicial accountability, corruption control, election-body autonomy and
impartial administration show major deterioration.
Media & expression: Decline in freedom of expression and media, with increased harassment of
journalists.
Academic freedom: Declines in academic expression, research/teaching freedom and institutional
autonomy.

What "Autocratisation" Means
Autocratisation is the gradual erosion of democratic institutions, as distinct from a sudden coup or
breakdown.
Important Facts for Exams
V-Dem — Varieties of Democracy Institute, at the University of Gothenburg, Sweden
Publishes the annual Democracy Report
India's Electoral Democracy Index at its lowest since 1975, the year the Emergency was declared
India described as undergoing slow autocratisation
Indicators at 50-year lows: judicial accountability, corruption, Election Management Body
autonomy, impartial public administration
Five V-Dem indices: Electoral, Liberal, Participatory, Deliberative, Egalitarian
The Electoral Democracy Index is based on the polyarchy concept of Robert Dahl
Other democracy indices: Freedom House "Freedom in the World", and the EIU Democracy Index
(which classifies countries as full democracy, flawed democracy, hybrid regime or authoritarian)
India's Emergency: 25 June 1975 to 21 March 1977, under Article 352
2. Mission Zero: Depalpur's Legacy Waste Transformation
Context Depalpur, Madhya Pradesh, became the first city under the Swachh Shehar Jodi initiative to
achieve Zero Legacy Waste — remediating 1,250 metric tonnes of waste accumulated over five years,
under SBM-U 2.0.
What It Is
A scientific legacy-waste remediation and urban waste-management initiative
Undertaken by Depalpur Nagar Parishad with Indore Municipal Corporation
Under the Swachh Shehar Jodi initiative of Swachh Bharat Mission-Urban 2.0
Follows the "Each One Teach One" model — experienced mentor cities transfer waste-management
practices to mentee cities
Important Facts for Exams
Depalpur, Madhya Pradesh — first city under Swachh Shehar Jodi to achieve Zero Legacy Waste
Mentor city: Indore Municipal Corporation
Under Swachh Bharat Mission-Urban 2.0, launched 2021
Swachh Shehar Jodi follows the "Each One Teach One" model
1,250 MT legacy waste remediated; 4.5 tonnes recyclables recovered; 50–60 tonnes RDF
RDF — Refuse Derived Fuel, the combustible fraction of waste
Co-processing — using RDF as fuel in cement kilns
Bio-mining — excavating and separating legacy waste
SBM-U 2.0 aims at garbage-free cities, focusing on legacy dumpsite remediation and source
segregation
Solid Waste Management Rules, 2016 mandate source segregation
Indore has repeatedly ranked India's cleanest city in Swachh Survekshan
3. The Permanent Court of Arbitration and the Indus Waters Treaty
Source: Indian Express
Context The Permanent Court of Arbitration (PCA) ruled that the Indus Waters Treaty remains in force
and restricted certain works on the Ratle Hydroelectric Project. India rejected the ruling, calling the

September 2026 full compilation  ·  Page 20 of 191
“Every topper was once an aspirant who did not stop.”
WhatsApp channel: whatsapp.com/channel/0029Vao03aUADTO9sOoigE2F  ·  +91 87086 52887  ·  c4scourses.in  ·  free, share it on
arbitration illegally constituted and its decision null and void.
What the PCA Is
An intergovernmental organisation facilitating international arbitration, conciliation and fact-
finding
For disputes between states, state entities, intergovernmental organisations and private parties
Establishment
Founded in 1899 at the first Hague Peace Conference
Under the Convention for the Pacific Settlement of International Disputes
Convened by Czar Nicholas II of Russia
The convention was revised at the second Hague Peace Conference in 1907
Headquarters: the Peace Palace, The Hague, Netherlands
India and Pakistan: both have been Contracting Parties since 1950, adhering to the 1899 and 1907
Hague Conventions
The Indus Waters Treaty
Signed 1960, brokered by the World Bank.
The allocation:
Eastern Rivers — Ravi, Beas, Sutlej → India
Western Rivers — Indus, Jhelum, Chenab → primarily Pakistan
India permitted limited non-consumptive hydropower use on the Western Rivers
Important Facts for Exams
PCA — founded 1899, first Hague Peace Conference, under the Convention for the Pacific Settlement
of International Disputes; revised 1907
Headquarters: Peace Palace, The Hague — which it shares with the International Court of Justice
Not a standing court — it administers ad-hoc tribunals
India and Pakistan have been Contracting Parties since 1950
IWT signed 1960, brokered by the World Bank
Eastern Rivers (Ravi, Beas, Sutlej) to India; Western Rivers (Indus, Jhelum, Chenab) primarily to
Pakistan
Article IX provides the graded dispute mechanism: Permanent Indus Commission → Neutral Expert →
Court of Arbitration
Ratle — 850 MW project on the Chenab; Kishenganga — on a tributary of the Jhelum
Distinguish the PCA from the ICJ: the ICJ is the principal judicial organ of the UN, with 15 permanent
judges, hearing only state-versus-state cases

Social Issue
1. UNEP Charts "Overshoot, Peak and Decline" Pathway
Source: The Hindu
Context The United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) for the first time set out a detailed
"overshoot, peak and decline" pathway — warning that a breach of the 1.5°C global warming limit will
bring irreversible ecological losses that no adaptation could undo.
What the Pathway Is
It projects a trajectory where the world overshoots the Paris Agreement's central goal of limiting
warming to 1.5°C above pre-industrial levels
Which the report says is now unavoidable
The pathway seeks to hold peak temperatures after the overshoot as low as possible
And then bring it back down below the limit by 2100

September 2026 full compilation  ·  Page 25 of 191
“The page you skip today is the question you miss tomorrow.”
WhatsApp channel: whatsapp.com/channel/0029Vao03aUADTO9sOoigE2F  ·  +91 87086 52887  ·  c4scourses.in  ·  free, share it on
Key Figures to Remember
Scenario Projected warming
1.5°C breach "In the next few years"
If every country delivers its national climate plan and net-zero target Peak warming of 1.8°C
Current policies About 2.6°C by 2100
The Paris Agreement Context
Adopted at COP21, Paris, 2015; entered into force 2016
Goal: hold warming well below 2°C, and pursue efforts to limit it to 1.5°C
Operates through Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs), submitted every five years
The global stocktake assesses collective progress every five years
Important Facts for Exams
Report: "Limiting Overshoot", by UNEP
First detailed "overshoot, peak and decline" pathway from the UN
1.5°C breach expected "in the next few years"
Full delivery of pledges → 1.8°C peak; current policies → about 2.6°C by 2100
UNEP — headquartered at Nairobi, Kenya; established 1972 after the Stockholm Conference
UNEP also publishes the annual Emissions Gap Report and Adaptation Gap Report
UN Secretary-General: António Guterres
Paris Agreement, 2015 — well below 2°C, pursuing 1.5°C; operates through NDCs
Net-negative emissions are required to return below the limit after a peak
India's targets: net zero by 2070, 45 per cent emissions intensity reduction by 2030, 50 per cent non-
fossil capacity by 2030

1. NABARD-IMD MoU on Climate-Resilient Agriculture via DiCRA
Source: News reports
Context NABARD signed an MoU with the India Meteorological Department (IMD) to integrate agro-
meteorological data into the Data in Climate Resilient Agriculture (DiCRA) platform — improving
access to weather intelligence for farmers, lenders and rural stakeholders.
What IMD will provide
Weather forecasts — short, medium and extended range
Agro-advisories — crop-specific guidance based on expected weather
In-situ observations — actual measurements from ground stations
Alerts — for extreme events such as heavy rainfall, heatwaves and cold waves
All integrated into a dedicated Weather Portal within DiCRA
How it will be delivered
Web portals — for institutional users
Mobile interfaces — for farmers and field staff
APIs — allowing banks, insurers and agri-tech firms to pull data into their own systems
Designed for open, user-friendly access and last-mile delivery
About DiCRA
Data in Climate Resilient Agriculture — an open-source digital platform
Supported by NABARD and the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)
It served as the digital infrastructure for NABARD's National Climate Stack Innovation Challenge,
where SatSure, EXPERIQS and VARSAPRADAYA built village-level climate risk tools on it

1. The Public Insurance Registry (PIR)
Source: Business Standard
Context IRDAI issued a consultation paper proposing a Public Insurance Registry (PIR) — conceived as
Digital Public Infrastructure under the Sabka Bima Sabki Raksha (Amendment of Insurance Laws) Act,
2025.
What It Is
A population-scale, interoperable and non-exclusionary Digital Public Infrastructure
Designed as a common information exchange layer
Allowing authorised participants to discover, verify and share insurance data — without creating a
single centralised data warehouse
About
Regulator and anchor: IRDAI
Implementing body: proposed by transforming the existing Insurance Information Bureau (IIB) into a
wholly owned, not-for-profit subsidiary of IRDAI
Participants: insurers, reinsurers, intermediaries, policyholders, financial institutions, government
departments and research institutions
Important Facts for Exams
PIR — proposed by IRDAI as Digital Public Infrastructure
Under the Sabka Bima Sabki Raksha (Amendment of Insurance Laws) Act, 2025
Implementing body: the Insurance Information Bureau (IIB), to become a wholly owned not-for-
profit subsidiary of IRDAI
Federated model — data stays with source institutions; not a central warehouse
To integrate with vehicle, health, weather, disaster and court databases
IRDAI — established under the IRDA Act, 1999, headquartered at Hyderabad
Comparable architecture: the RBI's Account Aggregator framework
2. SEBI Grants Category I Merchant Banking Licence to Zerodha
Source: BS
Context SEBI granted a Category-I merchant banking licence to Zerodha Corporate Advisors, a
subsidiary of Zerodha — enabling its entry into investment banking with a focus on capital market
services.
Category-I merchant bankers
Can manage main-board public issues and undertake underwriting
Minimum net worth: ₹50 crore — raised from the earlier ₹5 crore
Business capped at 20 times liquid net worth
Category-II merchant bankers
Cannot manage main-board public issues
Minimum net worth: ₹10 crore

1. The SCO Bishkek Declaration
Source: TOI
Context Leaders of the 10 SCO member states — including India, Russia and China — concluded the 26th
Council of Heads of State summit in Bishkek, Kyrgyzstan, by signing the joint Bishkek Declaration.
The Summit
Host: Bishkek, Kyrgyzstan
Chair: President Sadyr Japarov of the Kyrgyz Republic
Theme (2026): "25 Years of the SCO: Together for Sustainable Peace, Development and Prosperity"
Presidency handover: to Pakistan for the 2026-2027 tenure
Key Outcomes of the SCO Declaration
No double standards on terrorism: Condemned terrorism in all forms; called for action against cross-
border terrorism, safe havens and terror financing.
No extremist proxies: Rejected the use of terrorist/separatist groups for strategic purposes and
stressed state responsibility.
Iran strikes condemned: Opposed unilateral military strikes on Iran and called for diplomacy.
SCO is not a military bloc: Reaffirmed sovereignty, non-interference and that SCO is not directed
against any country.
Energy & food security: Warned that Strait of Hormuz disruptions could threaten global energy and
food security.
Financial cooperation: Supported national-currency trade, alternative payment systems and
discussions on an SCO Development Bank.
Connectivity: Supported CKU Railway and INSTC, while stressing sovereignty and territorial
integrity—important for India’s position on BRI/CPEC.
Youth & innovation: Institutionalised the SCO Young Scientists Forum and Start-up Forum.
Important Facts for Exams
26th SCO Council of Heads of State summit, Bishkek, Kyrgyzstan
Chair: President Sadyr Japarov; presidency to Pakistan for 2026-27
Theme: "25 Years of the SCO"

2. Earth Observation Satellite EOS-05
Source: TOI
Context ISRO has slated the launch of EOS-05, formerly GISAT-1A, aboard the GSLV-F17 rocket from
Sriharikota. It is the operational replacement for the lost GISAT-1 mission.
What It Is
An ISRO geo-imaging satellite built on the I-2K platform
Operating in Geosynchronous Orbit, about 35,786 km
Enabling near-real-time monitoring of India, surrounding oceans and border regions
About
Launcher: GSLV-F17 (GSLV Mk II), from the Second Launch Pad at SDSC-SHAR, Sriharikota
Why the high orbit: it matches Earth's rotation, overcoming the multi-day revisit gaps typical of Low
Earth Orbit satellites.
Primary objective: persistent spatial, spectral and thermal monitoring over the Indian landmass,
oceans and border zones — for disaster mitigation, agriculture and strategic defence.

2. UNEP Limiting Overshoot Report
Source: TH
Context
UNEP released Limiting Overshoot: Navigating Exceedance of 1.5°C and Pathways Towards Return. It
examines how the world can return below 1.5°C if global temperatures temporarily exceed the target.
What is Climate Overshoot?
Climate overshoot means global temperature temporarily exceeds 1.5°C, reaches a peak, and later
declines.
The pathway has four stages:
Exceedance1.
Plateau/Peak2.
Decline3.
Stabilization4.
Key Findings
Human-induced warming is already around 1.4°C and rising by about 0.25°C per decade.
WMO estimates a 75% probability that the 2026–2030 average will exceed 1.5°C.
Only around 130 GtCO₂ remains in the carbon budget for a 50% chance of limiting warming to 1.5°C.
At current emissions, this budget could be exhausted in roughly 3 years.

September 2026 full compilation  ·  Page 40 of 191
“Every day you show up, the syllabus gets smaller.”
WhatsApp channel: whatsapp.com/channel/0029Vao03aUADTO9sOoigE2F  ·  +91 87086 52887  ·  c4scourses.in  ·  free, share it on
Current policies could lead to around 2.6°C warming by 2100.
Even full implementation of NDCs and net-zero pledges could result in peak warming of about 1.8°C.
Cost of Delay
Every 5 years of continued high emissions could add around 0.1°C to peak warming.
Returning to lower temperatures would then require removing roughly 220 GtCO₂ from the
atmosphere.
Major Risks
Tipping points: Greenland and West Antarctic ice-sheet loss, AMOC weakening and Amazon dieback.
Glaciers: Mountain glaciers could lose more than 25% of their mass by 2100.
Sea level: Each century spent above 1.5°C could contribute around 40 cm of additional sea-level rise
by 2300.
Food security: Food production could decline by up to 14% by 2050 without adequate adaptation.
Coral reefs: Severe and repeated bleaching could push reefs beyond their ability to adapt.
Key Challenges & Way Forward
Key Challenges Way Forward
Debt stress: Climate disasters raise reconstruction
costs and borrowing pressures in vulnerable
countries.
Combine mitigation + adaptation: Treat both as
complementary, not alternatives.
Carbon removal limits: DACCS and BECCS need
significant energy, water and land; nature-based
removal faces saturation and wildfire risks.
Accelerate renewables & fossil-fuel phase-down: Reduce
dependence on fossil fuels rapidly.
Maladaptation: Poorly designed adaptation can
increase environmental and social vulnerability.
Rapid methane reduction: Prioritise methane cuts because
of its high short-term warming impact.
SIDS vulnerability: Tuvalu and Kiribati face sea-
level rise, saltwater intrusion and loss of habitable
land.
Strong MRV: Strengthen Measurement, Reporting and
Verification for carbon-removal projects.
Heat stress: Rising humid heat threatens labour
productivity and economic output.
Carbon removal as a supplement: It should complement,
not replace, direct emission reductions.
Climate-resilient infrastructure: Build resilient infrastructure
and plan retreat where unavoidable.
Loss & Damage finance: Strengthen financial support for
vulnerable developing countries.

3. ISRO Launches Earth Observation Satellite EOS-05
Source: ET
Context ISRO successfully launched its advanced Earth observation satellite EOS-05 aboard the GSLV-
F17 on 4 September 2026 — India's first imaging satellite designed to monitor Earth from
geosynchronous orbit, about 36,000 km up.
EOS-05: India’s First Dedicated Geosynchronous Earth-Imaging Satellite
ISRO successfully launched EOS-05 using the GSLV-F17 on 4 September 2026.
It is India’s first Earth-imaging satellite designed for geosynchronous orbit.
The satellite weighs around 2,367 kg, making it the heaviest payload launched by GSLV so far.
EOS-05 is also known as GISAT-1A.
Why is EOS-05 important?
Unlike conventional Earth-observation satellites in Low Earth Orbit (LEO), EOS-05 will operate from
geosynchronous orbit, around 36,000 km above Earth.
This allows it to repeatedly observe the same geographical region, providing frequent and near-real-
time imagery.
This capability is particularly useful for:
Disaster management
Agriculture
Forestry
Weather and environmental monitoring
Land-use monitoring
Strategic and national-security applications
GSLV-F17
Launch vehicle: GSLV Mk II / GSLV-F17
Developed and operated by: ISRO
Launch site: Satish Dhawan Space Centre, Sriharikota
The mission also demonstrated improved reliability of the GSLV Mk II.

1. Ophiopogon japonicus Recorded in India for the First Time
Source: Times of India
Context A medicinal plant traditionally used in East Asian healthcare, Ophiopogon japonicus, has been
recorded in India for the first time — discovered in West Kameng district, Arunachal Pradesh.
The Discovery
Recorded at an elevation of 2,185 metres in West Kameng district, Arunachal Pradesh
Documented by researchers from the Wildlife Institute of India (Dehradun) and the Uluberia
Botanical Institute Herbarium, West Bengal
Described as a significant find highlighting the biodiversity of the Eastern Himalaya
About the Species
Common names: dwarf lilyturf, mondo grass, fountain plant, monkey grass
Japanese names: ryū-no-hige ("dragon's beard") and ja-no-hige ("snake's beard")
Family: Asparagaceae; order Asparagales — traditionally placed in the lily family, Liliaceae
An evergreen, sod-forming, rhizomatous perennial
Linear, grass-like leaves
Has tuberous roots, which are the medicinally used part
Distribution
Native to China, Japan, Korea, Nepal and Vietnam
Now confirmed in India, widely grown as an ornamental ground-cover plant worldwide

What It Is

1. The Offline Verification Seeking Entity (OVSE) Framework
Source: New Indian Express
Context The Punjab School Education Board (PSEB) has become India's first school education board,
and Maharishi Dayanand University (MDU), Rohtak, the first university, to onboard UIDAI's Offline
Verification Seeking Entity (OVSE) framework.
What OVSE Is
A secure regulatory framework established by the UIDAI
Authorising eligible organisations to carry out local, offline identity verification
Using verifiable digital credentials, rather than querying central identity databases
Nodal body: UIDAI, under the Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology
Aim
Provide a tamper-evident, contactless and privacy-preserving identity verification ecosystem
Reduce operational costs
Eliminate physical document submission
Prevent identity fraud across sectors

1. Action Plan for Strengthening Krishi Vigyan Kendras
Source: PIB
Context Union Minister for Agriculture and Farmers' Welfare and Rural Development Shivraj Singh

Kendras (KVKs) through enhanced State participation — focused on implementing recommendations
from the 5th Chief Secretaries' Conference 2025.
About KVKs
Krishi Vigyan Kendras — district-level farm science centres, funded by ICAR and hosted by State
Agricultural Universities, ICAR institutes, NGOs and state departments
The first KVK was established at Puducherry in 1974
Coordinated regionally through ATARIs — Agricultural Technology Application Research Institutes
Mandate: on-farm testing, frontline demonstrations, capacity building of farmers and extension
personnel, and serving as knowledge and resource centres
Focus Areas
Crop production
Horticulture
Animal husbandry
Fisheries
Soil and water management
Farm machinery and technology
Value addition and post-harvest management
2. Next-Gen Aquaculture: RAS and Biofloc Technology
Source: PIB
Context A PIB feature on how Recirculatory Aquaculture Systems (RAS) and Biofloc technology are
transforming Indian aquaculture under PMMSY, advancing the Blue Economy and the Atmanirbhar
Bharat and Viksit Bharat visions.
India's Position in Global Fisheries
Second-largest fish producer — about 8 per cent of global fish production
Second-largest aquaculture producer
Largest producer and exporter of shrimp
Second-largest in capture fisheries

September 2026 full compilation  ·  Page 53 of 191
“Make notes you will actually read again.”
WhatsApp channel: whatsapp.com/channel/0029Vao03aUADTO9sOoigE2F  ·  +91 87086 52887  ·  c4scourses.in  ·  free, share it on
Supports the livelihoods of nearly three crore fishers and fish farmers
What is RAS?
RAS = Recirculatory Aquaculture System
Fish are raised in a controlled environment.
Water is continuously filtered, treated and reused.
Can recycle up to 90–95% of water.
What is Biofloc Technology?
Uses beneficial microorganisms to improve water quality.
Microbes convert fish waste and excess nutrients into microbial biomass.
This biomass can also become an additional food source for fish.
Helps reduce water exchange and improve resource efficiency.
RAS vs Biofloc — Simple Difference
RAS: Mainly depends on mechanical and biological water filtration + recirculation.
Biofloc: Mainly depends on beneficial microbes to manage waste and water quality.

1. Osbeckia ulotricha: New Plant Species Discovered in Western Ghats
Scientists from the Botanical Survey of India (BSI) discovered the new flowering plant species
Osbeckia ulotricha in the Agasthyamala Biosphere Reserve.
It occurs in shola grasslands and montane ecosystems of Kerala and Tamil Nadu at around
1,200–1,500 metres elevation.
It belongs to the Melastomataceae family and has distinctive morphological features.
The discovery highlights the floral biodiversity and conservation importance of the Western Ghats.
2. IMF G20 Report on Strong, Sustainable, Balanced and Inclusive Growth
The IMF's latest SSBIG report estimates medium-term G20 growth at only 3%, close to its weakest level
since the framework began in 2009.
Around half of G20 advanced economies face growth constraints because of restrictive labour,
product or consumer-market regulations.
Nearly three-fourths of G20 emerging-market economies also face regulatory constraints.
The report stresses regulatory and institutional reforms to encourage private investment, public
investment and long-term economic growth.

stakeholders.

corridors.

1. FATF Report on Tech-Driven Hawala Networks
The Financial Action Task Force (FATF) highlighted the transformation of traditional hawala networks
into technology-enabled systems.
The combination of virtual assets and hawala has created what is described as “digital hawala”,
increasing risks of money laundering and terrorist financing.
Nearly 70% of surveyed jurisdictions reported the use of new technologies in underground banking
and hawala-type networks.
FATF stressed stronger investigation, supervision and international cooperation to tackle professional
money laundering and underground banking.

will lapse.

Context Ahead of the 18th BRICS Summit in New Delhi, Chinese Ambassador Xu Feihong proposed a

China coordination, and collaboration on AI and supply chains.
The Context of Succession
India chairs BRICS in 2026; China takes over in 2027
The framework is pitched as guiding political, financial, technological and developmental
cooperation across the two consecutive presidencies
BRICS has evolved from an economic dialogue forum into a leading multilateral platform for the
Global South, offering an alternative to Western-dominated institutions such as the G-7
Key Figures
Greater BRICS encompasses nearly half the global population
Contributes about 30 per cent of global GDP, with a PPP share exceeding one-third
Accounts for one-fifth of global trade turnover, controlling production nodes in electronics, steel,
critical minerals and agriculture

continuity; push reforms of Bretton Woods institutions and
UNSC
2. UK Recognises India's Carbon Credit Trading Scheme
Source: ET
Context The United Kingdom has recognised India's Carbon Credit Trading Scheme (CCTS) as a
qualifying criterion for pricing relief under its Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism (CBAM) — a move
expected to reduce the tax burden on Indian exporters.
What CBAM Is
A carbon border tax levied on imports of carbon-intensive goods
Its purpose is to prevent carbon leakage — production shifting to countries with weaker climate rules
to avoid domestic carbon costs
The importer pays a charge reflecting the carbon embedded in the goods, equalising treatment with
domestically produced goods that already bear a carbon price
Typically covers iron and steel, aluminium, cement, fertilisers, hydrogen and electricity
Why Recognition Reduces the Liability
CBAM is designed to charge for carbon that has not already been priced in the country of origin
If an exporter has already paid a carbon price at home, that amount is deducted from the border
charge
Recognition of the CCTS means carbon costs paid under India's own scheme now count as a credit
against UK CBAM liability
Without recognition, Indian exporters would have paid twice — once under CCTS domestically and
again at the UK border
About India's Carbon Credit Trading Scheme
Notified in 2023 under the Energy Conservation (Amendment) Act, 2022
Administered by the Bureau of Energy Efficiency (BEE) under the Ministry of Power
Has two mechanisms:
Compliance mechanism — obligated entities in energy-intensive sectors must meet emission-
intensity targets
Offset mechanism — non-obligated entities can register projects and earn tradeable credits
Succeeds the earlier PAT (Perform, Achieve and Trade) scheme
Credits are traded on power exchanges, with the Grid Controller of India as registry and CERC as
regulator

1. IMF Warns of Weak Medium-Term G20 Growth
The IMF estimates medium-term G20 economic growth at around 3%, highlighting a weak global
growth outlook.
Restrictive regulations in labour, product and consumer markets are limiting growth in several G20
economies.
Emerging-market economies particularly face institutional and regulatory weaknesses that
discourage private and public investment.
The IMF calls for structural, regulatory and institutional reforms to improve productivity and
investment.
2. Zero-Emission Freight Push Through PACT
The PACT platform seeks to accelerate the adoption of zero-emission trucks in India's freight sector.
It aggregates freight demand and converts it into opportunities for deployment across specific freight
corridors.
The initiative connects shippers, LSPs, manufacturers, financiers and charging-point operators.
It supports India's broader transition towards cleaner and more sustainable transport.
3. Bisleri–SBI Partnership for Plastic Waste Management
Bisleri International partnered with State Bank of India (SBI) under the Bottles for Change initiative.
The partnership promotes segregation, collection and recycling of used plastic.
Awareness and training programmes will be conducted for SBI employees and housekeeping staff.
The initiative supports India's transition towards a circular economy and sustainable workplace
practices.

1. UNGA Declares November 27 as International Day Against Child Marriage
The United Nations General Assembly adopted a resolution declaring November 27 as the
International Day for the Elimination of Child, Early and Forced Marriage.
India was among the 70 co-sponsors of the resolution, which was adopted without a vote.
The first observance will take place on 27 November 2026.
Countries are encouraged to develop multisectoral national strategies, action plans, laws and
policies to eliminate child, early and forced marriage.

WhatsApp launched a native Bill Payments facility in India through the Bharat Connect network.
Users can pay electricity, gas, water, FASTag, insurance, credit-card and loan bills.
The service provides access to 22,722 billers across 30 categories.
Payments can be made using UPI, debit cards and credit cards, improving the convenience of digital
bill payments.

Reports & Articles
1. India's Per Capita Protein Consumption to Triple by 2070
Source: Business Standard
Context A report by Asia Research and Engagement (ARE) with FICCI projects India's per capita protein
consumption will more than triple by 2070, pushing protein-related emissions to more than seven times
the estimated climate-safe threshold.
The Consumption Projection
Per capita annual protein consumption: 9.5 kg (2025) → 30 kg (2070)
Dairy forms a significant part of the total
Drivers: population growth, rising incomes, urbanisation, public nutrition programmes, and shifting
dietary preferences
Despite the rise, India remains protein insecure and faces dietary inequality
Important Facts for Exams
Report by Asia Research and Engagement (ARE) with FICCI
Protein consumption: 9.5 kg (2025) → 30 kg (2070) per capita per year
BAU emissions 1.92 Gt CO₂e; climate-safe threshold 0.25 Gt CO₂e; combined approach 0.30 Gt CO₂e
India imports about 90 per cent of processed plant-protein isolates
Dairy and pulses to remain the main protein sources; red meat to stay small
India is the world's largest milk producer, about 24 per cent of global output
Enteric fermentation in ruminants is the largest agricultural methane source
Methane accounts for about 0.5°C of global warming and has a short atmospheric lifetime of about
a decade
Pulses fix atmospheric nitrogen, requiring far less fertiliser than animal protein systems
Protein isolate — protein extracted and concentrated from a plant source such as soy or pea

To be shifted to Nandankanan Zoological Park, Bhubaneswar, for quarantine

Banking and Finance
1. Coastal Local Area Bank Gets Scheduled Bank Status
The RBI included Coastal Local Area Bank Limited, based in Vijayawada, in the Second Schedule of
the RBI Act, 1934.
This gives the bank the status of a Scheduled Bank.
Coastal Local Area Bank was India’s first Local Area Bank (LAB).
Scheduled status allows access to RBI liquidity facilities and money-market operations.
Despite the new status, it will continue to operate as a Local Area Bank.
2. ADB Signs First Transaction Advisory Services Mandate in India with Madhya Pradesh
The Asian Development Bank (ADB) signed its first Transaction Advisory Services (TAS) mandate in
India with the Madhya Pradesh Government on 7 September 2026.
The objective is to develop renewable-energy projects through Public-Private Partnerships (PPPs).
The projects could attract up to US$1 billion in private investment.
ADB will assist in project preparation, financial structuring, procurement and tender development.
A solar power facility with Battery Energy Storage System (BESS) at Shajapur is among the projects
being developed.
3. Bandhan Bank and Mastercard Launch Four-Tier Credit Card Portfolio
Bandhan Bank and Mastercard launched a four-tier credit card portfolio.
The four variants are Sparks, Ignite, Flare and Lumina.
The cards provide benefits across grocery, apparel, travel, dining, entertainment and international
spending.
Customers can apply through digital channels as well as bank branches.
The initiative aims to expand Bandhan Bank’s presence in the retail credit-card segment.
4. Ministry of Finance Secures US$1 Billion IFC Funding for MSME Credit
The Ministry of Finance secured US$1 billion in funding support from the International Finance
Corporation (IFC) to strengthen long-term credit availability for MSMEs.
US$500 million has already been provided to SIDBI, while another US$500 million will come through a
lender syndicate.
The funds were converted into Indian rupees through the RBI’s special swap window.
SIDBI can provide on-lending for up to seven years.
Key beneficiary sectors include automobiles, engineering, electronics, textiles and food processing.

2. OPEC+
Source: News on Air
Context Seven core OPEC+ nations decided in a virtual meeting to keep their crude oil output targets
unchanged for October — amid global economic uncertainty and continuing supply disruptions across
the Middle East and the Strait of Hormuz.
What OPEC+ Is
An expanded coalition of OPEC member states and key non-OPEC oil-exporting nations
Working collectively to regulate crude oil supply and manage global energy markets
Formed in late 2016 through the Declaration of Cooperation, signed in Vienna, Austria
Membership
OPEC members include Saudi Arabia, Iraq, Kuwait, Algeria, Nigeria, Venezuela and others
Non-OPEC partners include Russia, Kazakhstan, Oman, Azerbaijan, Bahrain, Brunei, Malaysia,
Mexico, South Sudan and Sudan
Important Facts for Exams
OPEC founded 1960, at the Baghdad Conference; headquartered at Vienna, Austria
Founding five: Iran, Iraq, Kuwait, Saudi Arabia, Venezuela
OPEC+ formed late 2016 via the Declaration of Cooperation, Vienna
Trigger for OPEC+: the US shale surge of 2014–16 crashing prices
JMMC — Joint Ministerial Monitoring Committee, which reviews compliance and market conditions
Seven Sisters — the dominant Western oil majors before OPEC
Angola exited OPEC in 2024; Qatar left in 2019; Indonesia suspended membership
Strait of Hormuz — between Iran and Oman, connecting the Persian Gulf to the Gulf of Oman; roughly
a fifth of global oil passes through it
Other chokepoints: Strait of Malacca, Bab el-Mandeb, Suez Canal, Panama Canal, Turkish Straits
India imports over 85 per cent of its crude; major suppliers include Iraq, Saudi Arabia, Russia and the
UAE
India is a member of the International Energy Agency (IEA) as an association country, not OPEC
India maintains Strategic Petroleum Reserves at Visakhapatnam, Mangaluru and Padur

3. India's Carbon Credit Trading Scheme (CCTS)
Source: Times of India
Context The United Kingdom officially recognised India's Carbon Credit Trading Scheme (CCTS) as a
qualifying carbon pricing framework under its Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism (CBAM) —
reducing the effective CBAM liability on Indian exports.
What CCTS Is
A national market-based mechanism establishing the Indian Carbon Market (ICM)
Prices greenhouse gas emissions, issues tradable Carbon Credit Certificates (CCCs), and drives
industrial decarbonisation
Notified June 2023 under the Energy Conservation (Amendment) Act, 2022
Detailed compliance regulations adopted July 2024
Aim
Accelerate decarbonisation of the Indian economy
Fulfil India's Nationally Determined Contributions under the Paris Agreement
Incentivise emission cuts in energy-intensive sectors through a transparent carbon-trading
exchange

Agriculture
1. High-Productivity Crop Diversification and Horticulture
Source: Sansad TV
Context The Union Budget 2026-27 emphasised a crop-specific, regionally differentiated agricultural
policy framework to accelerate high-productivity crop diversification — moving beyond cereal
monocropping toward high-value horticulture, plantation crops and agroforestry.
What Crop Diversification Means
Transitioning land use from monoculture systems — notably the water-intensive paddy-wheat
rotation — toward pulses, oilseeds, horticulture and commercial tree crops
Horticulture is the cornerstone, generating higher GVA per unit of land, optimising water use, and
building climate resilience
Important Facts for Exams
Decadal agricultural growth: 4.45 per cent
Horticulture: 37 per cent of crop sub-sector gross value output; production 370.74 mt (2024-25) —
vegetables 217.80, fruits 117.65, others 35.29
India is second globally in fruits and vegetables; first in onions and shallots (22.42 per cent)
Horticulture production has exceeded foodgrain production since 2012-13
Coconut: second in output (22.44 per cent), third in area; 13.97 mt across 2.19 mn ha; yield 6.36 t/ha
Kerala — largest area; Tamil Nadu — highest production; Andhra Pradesh — highest yield
Coconut Development Board — headquartered at Kochi, under the Ministry of Agriculture
Agarwood — from Aquilaria species; yields oud oil; nearly 150 million trees, over 90 per cent in the
Northeast; regulated under CITES Appendix II
Walnut: 3.22 lakh tonnes (2024-25); exports $7.80 million; mainly from Jammu & Kashmir
MIDH — Mission for Integrated Development of Horticulture, the main horticulture scheme
2. PM-KUSUM 2.0 Likely to Be Announced Soon
Source: The Hindu
Context Union Minister for New and Renewable Energy Pralhad Joshi said the second phase of PM-
KUSUM is likely to be announced shortly — speaking at the curtain raiser for the 7th Agrivoltaics World
Conference 2026.
What Agrivoltaics Is
Combining agricultural activity with solar power generation on the same land
Panels are mounted raised or spaced so crops grow beneath or between them
The land yields both a crop and electricity, instead of one or the other

About PM-KUSUM
Pradhan Mantri Kisan Urja Suraksha evam Utthaan Mahabhiyan, launched 2019
Under the Ministry of New and Renewable Energy
Three components:
Component A — decentralised ground-mounted grid-connected solar plants on barren or fallow
farmland, up to 2 MW each
Component B — installation of standalone solar pumps to replace diesel pumps
Component C — solarisation of existing grid-connected agriculture pumps, including feeder-level
solarisation
Important Facts for Exams
PM-KUSUM launched 2019, under the Ministry of New and Renewable Energy; full form: Pradhan
Mantri Kisan Urja Suraksha evam Utthaan Mahabhiyan
PM-KUSUM 2.0 to add a dedicated agrivoltaics component
Union Minister, New and Renewable Energy: Pralhad Joshi
Agriculture accounts for a large share of India's subsidised power consumption; solar pumps cut both
the subsidy burden and diesel use
India's solar capacity rose from about 3 GW (2016) to over 160 GW (mid-2026)

1. The Asian-Pacific Postal Union (APPU)
Source: PIB
Context Dr Vinaya Prakash Singh, a senior Indian Postal Service (IPoS) officer, was re-elected
unopposed by acclamation as Secretary-General of the APPU for a second consecutive four-year term,
2027–2030.
What APPU Is
An intergovernmental organisation of 32 member countries across the Asia-Pacific
Functions as a Restricted Union — a regional union — under Article 8 of the Universal Postal Union
(UPU) Constitution
The UPU is a specialised agency of the United Nations
Headquartered at Bangkok, Thailand — relocated from Manila in 2002
Aim
Extend, facilitate and improve postal relations among member states
Coordinate cross-border logistics and regulatory standards
Promote technical cooperation and modernisation of postal networks across the Asia-Pacific

32 member countries; headquartered at Bangkok, moved from Manila in 2002

2. Mount Anak Krakatau
Source: The Print
Context A series of intense volcanic eruptions at Indonesia's Mount Anak Krakatau blanketed Jakarta
and surrounding provinces in dense ash clouds, forcing suspension of flights at Jakarta airport.
What It Is
An active volcanic island and stratovolcano, featuring a pyroclastic cone
The name means "Child of Krakatoa" in Indonesian
Formation and Origin
Emerged within the marine caldera created by the catastrophic 1883 VEI-6 eruption of Krakatoa,
which destroyed two-thirds of the original island
Submarine eruptive activity began in early 1927
Repeated cycles of basaltic accumulation breached the ocean surface, rising permanently above
sea level on 11 August 1930
Important Facts for Exams
Anak Krakatau — "Child of Krakatoa"; in the Sunda Strait, between Java and Sumatra, Indonesia
Emerged from the caldera of the 1883 Krakatoa eruption (VEI-6); rose above sea level on 11 August
1930
Lies within Ujung Kulon National Park, a UNESCO World Heritage Site and last refuge of the Javan
rhinoceros
On the Pacific Ring of Fire; about 700 km north of the Sunda Trench
Indo-Australian Plate subducts beneath the Sunda Plate — a convergent boundary
December 2018 flank collapse caused the Sunda Strait tsunami — a volcanogenic tsunami, not
seismic, which is why no earthquake warning preceded it
VEI — Volcanic Explosivity Index, a 0–8 logarithmic scale
Eruption types: Strombolian (mild, frequent bursts), Vulcanian (more explosive, ash-rich), Plinian
(most violent)
Stratovolcano — a steep, layered cone of lava and pyroclastic material; Somma volcano — a new
cone growing inside an older caldera
Indonesia has the most active volcanoes of any country; other notable ones: Merapi, Tambora,
Semeru, Sinabung

Agriculture
1. UNCCD COP17
Source: UNCCD
Context The 17th Conference of the Parties (COP17) to the UNCCD concluded in Ulaanbaatar, Mongolia,
under the theme "Restoring Land, Restoring Hope" — focused on rangeland conservation, drought
financing and community-led restoration, amid deadlock over a binding global drought treaty.
About the UNCCD
Established following the 1992 Rio Earth Summit
The sole legally binding international agreement linking environment and development to
sustainable land management
COP17 coincided with the UN International Year of Rangelands and Pastoralists, placing pastoral
communities and fragile open ecosystems at the centre of the Land Degradation Neutrality (LDN)
agenda
Important Facts for Exams
UNCCD COP17 — Ulaanbaatar, Mongolia; theme "Restoring Land, Restoring Hope"
UNCCD — adopted Paris, 17 June 1994; one of the three Rio Conventions with the UNFCCC and CBD;
the only legally binding agreement on land management; 197 Parties
LDN corresponds to SDG target 15.3
UNCCD's finance arm: the Global Mechanism; India hosted COP14 at New Delhi in 2019
40 per cent of global land degraded; Mongolia 77 per cent
Drought becomes a standalone agenda item from COP18
DRI — Drought Resilience Index; DRIF — Drought Resilience Investment Facility, co-launched with
Luxembourg
About 30 per cent of India's land is degraded (SAC-ISRO Atlas)
Orans — sacred groves of Rajasthan, community-conserved
2026 is the International Year of Rangelands and Pastoralists

3. Proposed Article 371(K) for Ladakh
Source: Indian Express
Context The Ministry of Home Affairs proposed inserting a new constitutional provision — Article 371(K),
as Chapter K under Part XXI — to establish a directly elected governing body with legislative powers for
the Union Territory of Ladakh.
What It Is
A proposed amendment under Part XXI — Temporary, Transitional and Special Provisions
Creating a sui generis (unique) governance model for Ladakh
Providing a directly elected UT-level governing body with legislative authority over specified
subjects
Background
Under the J&K Reorganisation Act, 2019, Ladakh was carved out as a Union Territory without a
legislative assembly
Governance became led largely by bureaucrats and the Lieutenant Governor rather than elected
representatives
The existing Autonomous Hill Development Councils — LAHDC Leh and LAHDC Kargil — were sidelined
The Leh Apex Body (LAB) and Kargil Democratic Alliance (KDA) joined forces, demanding full
statehood, Sixth Schedule protection, dedicated public service commissions and parliamentary
representation
Aim
Restore democratic governance in Ladakh
Protect tribal rights, land and ecology
Address political alienation — without granting statehood or Sixth Schedule status

1. CCEA Approves Eight Railway Multitracking Projects Worth ₹20,804 Crore
The Cabinet Committee on Economic Affairs (CCEA) approved eight railway multitracking projects
costing ₹20,804 crore.
The projects cover 31 districts across nine states.
Five projects worth ₹10,021 crore will cover Tamil Nadu, Andhra Pradesh, Karnataka and Telangana.
Three projects worth ₹10,783 crore will cover West Bengal, Jharkhand, Odisha, Madhya Pradesh and
Chhattisgarh, adding about 656 km to the railway network.

1. SEBI Launches Demat 2.0
Source: IE
Context SEBI launched Demat 2.0, a pilot project for tokenisation of corporate bonds using distributed
ledger technology (DLT) — jointly launched by RBI Governor Sanjay Malhotra and SEBI Chairman Tuhin
Kanta Pandey at the Global Fintech Fest.
What is Demat 2.0?
Demat = keeping your shares and securities in digital form instead of paper form.
Demat 1.0 = Paper shares → Digital shares
Demat 2.0 = Digital bonds → Tokenised bonds + Digital money settlement
India introduced Demat 1.0 in 1996, which converted physical securities into electronic form.
How It Works
Corporate bonds are created as digital tokens on a distributed ledger
A shared electronic record maintained by market infrastructure institutions using DLT
The ledger is owned by the depositories

payments.
Resorts World Sentosa is among the first merchant partners under the initiative.

1. DILRMP 3.0 Launched
Source: TOI
Context Union Minister Shivraj Singh Chouhan, Ministry of Rural Development, launched the Operational
Guidelines for DILRMP 3.0 — a Central Sector Scheme with an outlay of ₹565.50 crore for 2026–2031 — at
Krishi Bhawan, New Delhi.
The Core Objective
Build an integrated GIS-based digital land records ecosystem
Expected to improve citizen access to land-related services across the country
The Four Key Components
Bhu-Aadhaar (ULPIN)
Unique Land Parcel Identification Number
A 14-digit unique identifier for every land parcel in the country
Registration Seva Kendras (RSKs)
Upgradation of 75 high-footfall Sub-Registrar Offices
Modelled on Passport Seva Kendras
Revenue Court Case Management System (RCCMS)
Modernisation, by linking it directly with land records
NAKSHA
National Geospatial Knowledge-based Land Survey of Urban Habitations
Acceleration of the pilot to create accurate GIS-based urban land records
And issue Urban Property Cards (UrPro Cards)
2. NSO Releases First-Ever District-Level Estimates of the Unincorporated Non-Agricultural
Sector
Source: PIB
Context of the News
In September 2026, the National Statistical Office (NSO) under the Ministry of Statistics and
Programme Implementation (MoSPI) released the report “Unincorporated Sector at the District Level:
Insights from ASUSE 2025.” This is the first time India has received estimates of the unincorporated
non-agricultural sector at the district level, giving a much more detailed picture of enterprises, workers
and economic activity across different districts.

September 2026 full compilation  ·  Page 85 of 191
“Confidence comes from revision, not from hope.”
WhatsApp channel: whatsapp.com/channel/0029Vao03aUADTO9sOoigE2F  ·  +91 87086 52887  ·  c4scourses.in  ·  free, share it on
Key Highlights
Based on ASUSE 2025
The report uses data from the Annual Survey of Unincorporated Sector Enterprises (ASUSE) 2025.
Survey period: January–December 2025
It covers unincorporated non-agricultural enterprises operating across India.
The district-level estimates provide more granular data than state-level estimates.
Earlier, we could understand the unincorporated sector mainly at the state level. Now, we can see which
districts are contributing the most.
What is the Unincorporated Sector?
Unincorporated enterprises are businesses that are not registered as companies under the Companies
Act.
Examples include:
Small shops
Street vendors
Small manufacturing units
Repair businesses
Small restaurants
Household enterprises
Other small service and trading businesses
These enterprises are important because they provide large-scale employment and contribute
significantly to India's economy.
Top District by State
Telangana — Rangareddy, 23.53 per cent
Gujarat — Surat, 19.43 per cent
West Bengal — North 24 Parganas, 15.99 per cent
Punjab — Ludhiana, 14.74 per cent
Karnataka — Bengaluru (Urban), 12.83 per cent
Kerala — Thiruvananthapuram, 12.38 per cent
Rajasthan — Jaipur, 11.70 per cent
Maharashtra — Pune, 9.14 per cent
Bihar — Patna, 8.23 per cent
Tamil Nadu — Chennai, 7.13 per cent
Uttar Pradesh — Prayagraj, 5.77 per cent
Key Terms
NSO: National Statistical Office, the main statistical agency under MoSPI.
ASUSE: Annual Survey of Unincorporated Sector Enterprises.
Unincorporated Enterprise: A business that is not incorporated as a company under the Companies
Act.
GVA: Gross Value Added; the value added by producers to goods and services after accounting for
the cost of inputs.
Granular Data: Detailed information available at a smaller geographical level, such as the district
level.

4. Ladakh Glacial Water Initiative
In Chuchot and nearby villages of Ladakh, glacial meltwater was flowing unused into the Indus River,
while agricultural fields faced water shortages.
Officials identified a Stok village nallah, fed by the Stok Kangri Glacier, carrying nearly 90 million litres
of water daily.
Instead of constructing expensive new infrastructure, the water was connected to existing irrigation
canals through basic earthwork.
The initiative now provides irrigation to more than 1,000 acres across 12 villages, demonstrating
community-led water management.

monitoring.

The system will help in plantation mapping, yield estimation, crop-health assessment and pest and
disease forecasting.

NABARD  CURRENT  AFFAIRS
Agriculture
1. Assisted Reproductive Technologies in Bovine Breeding
Context India's dairy sector is increasingly using Assisted Reproductive Technologies (ART) to raise milk
productivity and conserve indigenous bovine genetics — speeding up genetic improvement beyond
what conventional breeding allows.
The ART Methods
Artificial Insemination (AI) — Artificial Insemination is the process of introducing semen from a
selected superior bull into the female reproductive tract without natural mating.
Oestrus Synchronisation — Oestrus synchronisation means using hormones to make a group of
females come into heat at approximately the same time.
MOET — MOET is used to obtain several embryos from a genetically superior female.
IVF — In Vitro Fertilisation; fertilisation outside the body under laboratory conditions
OPU — Ovum Pick-Up; collects oocytes from live donor females without surgery
Sexed semen — influences the sex ratio of calves, improving breeding efficiency
Cloning — Cloning produces an animal that is genetically almost identical to another animal. The
most well-known method is Somatic Cell Nuclear Transfer (SCNT).
India's Cloning Milestones
2009 — the world's first buffalo calf cloned using the Hand-guided Cloning Technique, at ICAR-NDRI,
Karnal
2025 — India's first cloned cattle, a Gir female calf named GANGA, produced at ICAR-NDRI using OPU
for oocyte collection and SCNT for embryo reconstruction
Scientists have also generated cloned Badri embryos, and are exploring cloning of elite high-
producing Badri cows
Indigenous Breeds
India has over 50 officially recognised indigenous cattle breeds
Valued for adaptability to local climates, disease resistance and suitability for low-input farming
Many produce A2 beta-casein milk
Important dairy breeds: Gir, Sahiwal, Tharparkar, Rathi, Red Sindhi, Kankrej, Hariana, Deoni, Ongole
Badri cattle — indigenous to Uttarakhand and recognised as the state animal; important for
Himalayan bovine diversity and mountain agriculture
Government Schemes
Rashtriya Gokul Mission (RGM) — launched December 2014, for development and conservation of
indigenous breeds, genetic upgradation and milk productivity. Interventions include the Nationwide

2. ICAR-IVRI Produces Sahiwal Calves Through OPU-IVF-ET
Source: ICAR
Context ICAR–Indian Veterinary Research Institute (IVRI), Izatnagar, Bareilly successfully produced
indigenous Sahiwal cattle calves using ultrasound-guided ovum pick-up, in vitro fertilisation and
embryo transfer (OPU–IVF–ET) — the first success of its kind at the institute.
The Programme
Began in 2022–23, to strengthen the genetic potential of India's prized dairy breeds
Aim: standardise OPU–IVF–ET protocols under both farm and field conditions
Target breeds: Sahiwal cattle, Tharparkar cattle and Murrah buffalo
OPU–IVF–ET
OPU–IVF–ET is an advanced Assisted Reproductive Technology (ART) used to rapidly multiply the
genetics of superior cattle and buffaloes.
OPU (Ovum Pick-Up): Eggs are collected from the ovaries of an elite female.
IVF (In Vitro Fertilisation): Eggs are fertilised with selected bull semen in the laboratory.
ET (Embryo Transfer): Developed embryos are transferred into synchronised recipient females.
Simple flow:
Elite female → OPU → IVF → Embryo → ET → Calf
Key benefit: Produces more offspring from genetically superior females in a shorter time.
3. Moth Bean
Source: PIB
Context Four newly developed moth bean varieties — CAZRI Moth-4, 5, 6 and 7 — by ICAR-Central Arid
Zone Research Institute (CAZRI) demonstrated remarkable field resilience during an El Niño year in

What Moth Bean Is
Vigna aconitifolia — also called mat bean, Turkish gram or dew bean
An extremely drought-hardy, herbaceous, short-day annual legume of the family Fabaceae
Among the most resilient pulse crops of hot arid and semi-arid zones of South Asia
Habitat and Growing Conditions
Native to South Asia; grown in hot, rainfed, marginal desert tracts
Most extensively in the Thar Desert region of western Rajasthan, and parts of Gujarat, Haryana and
Punjab
Thrives in low and erratic rainfall, poor sandy soils with low organic carbon and fertility, and
temperatures exceeding 40°C
Important Facts for Exams

Vigna aconitifolia — moth bean, mat bean, Turkish gram, dew bean; family Fabaceae
A short-day annual legume; among the most drought-tolerant pulses
Grown mainly in the Thar Desert, western Rajasthan; also Gujarat, Haryana, Punjab
Cultivated on nearly 10 lakh hectares; yield about 350 kg per hectare
Regional rainfall 150–250 mm; normal dry spells of 20–30 days
Varieties: CAZRI Moth-4, 5, 6 and 7, developed by ICAR-CAZRI, Jodhpur
Survived a 35-day dry spell (3 August – 7 September 2026) in Kharif 2026
Developmental plasticity — flowering at about 30 days; adjusts maturity to moisture
"Pala" — the dry fodder from moth bean, valued for desert livestock
Rhizobium symbiosis fixes atmospheric nitrogen in root nodules
Other Vigna pulses: moong (Vigna radiata), urad (Vigna mungo), cowpea (Vigna unguiculata)
ICAR-CAZRI, Jodhpur — the nodal institute for arid zone research
India is the world's largest producer and consumer of pulses, and also the largest importer
4. Schizopygopsis waddellii Recorded in India
Source: News on Air
Context Scientists from ICAR-Central Institute of Coldwater Fisheries Research (CIFRI-Coldwater), with
the Arunachal Pradesh Department of Fisheries, recorded the rare Tibetan fish Schizopygopsis
waddellii in India for the first time.
What It Is
A cold-adapted freshwater fish of the Cyprinidae family — specifically the snowtrout /
schizothoracine group
Confirmed as a new Indian record through an integrative taxonomic approach, combining
morphological examination with mitochondrial DNA (mtDNA) analysis
Habitat and Distribution
Native to high-altitude cold-water ecosystems of the Tibetan and Chinese plateaus
Type locality: Yamdok Lake, Tibet
In India, found in Pangkang Teng Tso Lake, at 12,800 feet, in Tawang district, Arunachal Pradesh
Extends its known range into the Eastern Himalaya

Banking and Finance
1. The Bankers' Books Evidence Act, 2026
Source: Business Standard
Context The Central Government notified that the Bankers' Books Evidence Act, 2026 — which received
Presidential assent on 13 August 2026 — will come into force from 1 October 2026.
What It Is
A statutory framework governing the admissibility, certification and legal validity of bank records
and accounts as prima facie evidence in court
Without requiring production of original physical registers
It replaces a 135-year-old law — the Bankers' Books Evidence Act, 1891

September 2026 full compilation  ·  Page 95 of 191
“Twenty facts a day is six hundred a month. Start with today's.”
WhatsApp channel: whatsapp.com/channel/0029Vao03aUADTO9sOoigE2F  ·  +91 87086 52887  ·  c4scourses.in  ·  free, share it on
Aim
Establish a technology-neutral regime for digital, virtual and cloud-based financial data
Streamline dispute resolution
Enhance Ease of Doing Business
Five Key Features
Technology-neutral recognition — covers physical ledgers, electronic media, digital entries, virtual
environments and cloud storage systems
Simplified certification — accepts verification through manual, digital or electronic signatures
Integrity conditions for admissibility — digital copies must be true representations or extractions,
show no unauthorised data alterations, and show no system tampering affecting data integrity
Statutory definition of "special cause" — bank officials cannot be routinely summoned, nor original
books compelled, where the bank is not a party, unless the court records a special cause in writing
Scope expansion — retains coverage of commercial banks and Post Office Savings Banks, while
empowering the Central Government to extend the Act to NBFCs, fintechs and other financial
institutions by notification
2. SEBI Proposes Changes to Closing Auction Session Framework
Source: The Hindu
Context SEBI issued a consultation paper proposing changes to derivatives settlement methodology,
market timings and operational aspects of the Closing Auction Session (CAS) — weeks after the new
closing price mechanism took effect. Seven proposals, with comments invited by 3 October 2026.
Key Proposals
Expiry-Day Settlement Price1.
Option 1: Use a blended VWAP from the last 30 minutes of continuous trading + 10-minute CAS.
Option 2: Continue using CTS VWAP based only on the last 30 minutes before CAS.
SEBI suggests retaining CTS VWAP for at least one year before considering a change.
Stop Indicative Index Value (IIV)2.
Security-level IEPs will continue, but the indicative index value during CAS may be discontinued.
Reason: IIV is based on unexecuted indicative prices, not actual trades.
Market Timings3.
Option A: CAS from 3:31–3:40 pm, derivatives until 3:45 pm.
Option B: CAS stocks trade until 3:15 pm, CAS from 3:15–3:25 pm, derivatives end at 3:30 pm.
Shorter Transition4.
Gap between continuous trading and CAS may be reduced from 5 minutes to about 1 minute.
Shorter Derivatives Window5.
Post-CAS derivatives trading may be reduced from 10 minutes to 5 minutes.
Order Cancellation Restriction6.
Orders within ±1% of the reference price can be cancelled.
Orders beyond 1% and up to 3% cannot be cancelled, but can be modified to improve their price.
Iceberg Orders7.
Unexecuted iceberg orders can move into CAS.
Their remaining quantity will become a normal limit order and the full quantity will be visible in the
CAS order book.

September 2026 full compilation  ·  Page 96 of 191
“Twenty facts a day is six hundred a month. Start with today's.”
WhatsApp channel: whatsapp.com/channel/0029Vao03aUADTO9sOoigE2F  ·  +91 87086 52887  ·  c4scourses.in  ·  free, share it on
Key Terms
CAS: Closing Auction Session used to discover the closing price through an auction.
IEP: Indicative Equilibrium Price; the price at which maximum executable quantity can potentially be
matched during an auction.
VWAP: Volume Weighted Average Price, where prices are weighted according to trading volume.
CTS VWAP: Closing Trading Session VWAP based on trades during the specified pre-CAS period.
IIV: Indicative Index Value calculated during CAS using indicative prices of index constituents.
Iceberg Order: An order where only part of the total quantity is normally visible to the market.
Price Discovery: The process through which buying and selling interests determine a market price.
